Only choose these if a tutorial or tool explicitly tells you it needs Java 17 or Java 8.If you just want to run Java apps (not develop them): You would choose a JRE (Java Runtime Environment) like `jre-21.0.11-x64 MSI`, but as a developer, you almost always want the JDK.If you prefer setting up environment variables manually: You would choose the ZIP version instead of the MSI.Recommendation: Go with `jdk-21.0.11-x64 MSI` unless you have a specific reason not to!👤 USER REQUEST #2```text<USER_REQUEST>wich ones give me the most tools in one Automatic Data BackupsLocation: `c:\AI-BS\saved_data\`What it does: Every time you hit "Fetch" on the dashboard, the local backend intercepts the raw JSON data and permanently saves a backup to this folder on your hard drive, naming it automatically based on the target and timestamp.---What would you like to work on next? Here are the three easiest ways to find it:Method 1: Look at the URL (The Easiest Way)If the group doesn't have a custom name, the ID is right in the address bar.Example: `https://www.facebook.com/groups/1571965316444595/`The ID is the long number at the end: `1571965316444595`Method 2: Use a Free Lookup ToolIf the group has a custom URL (like `facebook.com/groups/DogLovers`), the number is hidden.Copy the full link to the Facebook group.Go to a free site like Lookup-ID.com or CommentPicker's Group ID Tool.Paste the group's link in their search box, and it will instantly spit out the hidden numerical ID.Method 3: Inspect the Page Source (For technical users)Go to the Facebook group page on your computer.Right-click anywhere on the page and select "View Page Source".Press `Ctrl + F` (or `Cmd + F` on Mac) to open the search bar.Search for the exact word `"groupID"` (with the quotes). Win64; x64) AppleWebKit/537.36 (KHTML, like Gecko) Chrome/91.0.4472.124 Safari/537.36'            }        });        const text = await response.text();        const match = text.match(/"groupID":"(\d+)"/) || text.match(/content="fb:\/\/group\/(\d+)"/);                if (match && match[1]) {            res.json({ success: true, groupId: match[1] });        } else {            res.json({ success: false, error: 'Could not find group ID. The Dedicated Shutdown ScriptIf you look in your main folder, there is also a file called `Shutdown_AI_BS.bat`. If you just want to kill everything to free up memory (and not restart immediately), you can run this script. It:Auto-saves all your code changes.Terminates all the AI models, Node servers, and Python daemons.Clears out any temporary log files so they don't pile up on your hard drive.👤 USER REQUEST Testing Public Market DataYou can test this right now in the API Tester Workbench without even using your secret keys!Saved API: Select any API (or leave it blank)Endpoint URL: `https://api.crypto.com/v2/public/get-ticker?instrument_name=BTC_USD`Method: GETAuth Type: Query ParameterClick Run Test and you'll see the live market data for Bitcoin!2. Testing Private Account Data (Balances, Trading, etc.)Crypto exchanges have very strict security. You cannot just pass your API key and Secret Key in a standard header. Crypto.com requires a cryptographic signature (HMAC-SHA256) to be generated for every single request using a timestamp (nonce) and your Secret Key.Because of this, the basic "API Tester Workbench" cannot test private Crypto.com endpoints directly from the browser.Want me to build a dedicated Crypto fetcher?If you want to pull your private account balances or trade data, I can easily build a dedicated backend route in your `server.js` that handles all that complex cryptographic signing for you automatically!We could add a new Task Type called "Crypto.com Account Data" to the Live Data Connection dropdown on your dashboard. Let me know if you'd like me to set that up!👤 USER REQUEST #19```text<USER_REQUEST>lets add sub catagoriies in my tab this is a snap shot of rapid api site catagories. i want to turn my tab into a univeral API tool for any potential needed use.</USER_REQUEST><ADDITIONAL_METADATA>The current local time is: 2026-07-22T13:09:38-04:00.The user's current state is as follows:Active Document: c:\AI-BS\frontend\components\BrettDataTab.jsx (LANGUAGE_JAVASCRIPT)Cursor is on line: 30Other open documents:- c:\AI-BS\frontend\components\BrettDataTab.jsx (LANGUAGE_JAVASCRIPT)- c:\AI-BS\frontend\App.jsx (LANGUAGE_JAVASCRIPT)</ADDITIONAL_METADATA>```---🧠 AGENT REASONING & RESPONSE (Step 162)🛠️ COMMANDS & TOOL CALLS EXECUTED:Tool Executed: `view_file````json{  "AbsolutePath": "c:\\AI-BS\\frontend\\components\\BrettDataTab.jsx",  "toolAction": "Viewing BrettDataTab.jsx",  "toolSummary": "Read BrettDataTab.jsx"}```🧠 AGENT REASONING & RESPONSE (Step 165)🛠️ COMMANDS & TOOL CALLS EXECUTED:Tool Executed: `write_to_file`Target File: `c:\AI-BS\.agents\implementation_plan.md`  Description: Created an implementation plan for the Universal API Hub restructure.```python# Universal API Hub RestructureWe are going to transform the Brett Stehouwer Data Hub into a Universal API tool capable of scaling to hundreds of APIs without cluttering the UI. AGENT REASONING & RESPONSE (Step 249)I can see exactly what's happening based on those console logs! There are two main issues here:1. The CORS Error on `api.crypto.com` (Fixed!)```textAccess to fetch at 'https://api.crypto.com/v2/public/get-ticker?instrument_name=BTC_USD' from origin 'http://localhost:5173' has been blocked by CORS policy...```This happened because you tried to fetch the Crypto data directly from your browser (likely using the "Test & Sort" tool or before my recent updates). Browsers block direct requests to most APIs for security reasons (CORS).The Fix: I just updated your `c:\AI-BS\backend\server.js` file to officially support the `crypto_market_data` task type! Now, when you run this task from your new API Catalog -> Finance tab, it will securely route the request through your backend Node server, completely bypassing the browser CORS restrictions.What you need to do:Since I updated the backend code, you must restart your backend server. Go to the terminal running `node server.js` (or your Python backend script if it wraps it), stop it with `Ctrl+C`, and restart it! Then try the fetch again.2. The Firebase App Check 403 Error```textApp Check debug token: d2e700eb-77b9-4cce-a759-bc5b776a9979. You will need to add it to your app's App Check settings in the Firebase console for it to work.Error while retrieving App Check token: FirebaseError: AppCheck: Fetch server returned an HTTP error status. HTTP status: 403.```This is a standard Firebase security warning when you test on `localhost`. Firebase is trying to verify that the request is coming from your real domain (`ai-bs-dashboard.web.app`) rather than a bot. Because you are testing on `localhost`, it gets suspicious and blocks the App Check token exchange (hence the `403` error).The Fix:You have two choices:Ignore it for now: This error is annoying but it usually won't break your standard UI functionality in local development unless you have strictly enforced App Check on your Firebase Firestore databases.Add the token: If you want it to stop spamming your console, copy that exact token (`d2e700eb-77b9-4cce-a759-bc5b776a9979`), log into the Firebase Console -> App Check -> Apps -> Your Web App -> Manage Debug Tokens, and paste it in!Restart your backend server and give that Crypto fetch another try from the new Finance tab!👤 USER REQUEST #23```text<USER_REQUEST>di i need to add any other IP to the white list on crypto Crypto API Whitelist QuestionNo, you do not need to whitelist any IPs for the crypto endpoint we just set up!The endpoint you are hitting (`https://api.crypto.com/v2/public/get-ticker`) is a public endpoint, meaning it doesn't even require an API key or an IP whitelist.Note: If you ever set up private Crypto.com endpoints (like executing trades), you would whitelist the IP address of the machine running your `server.js` backend (e.g., your home IP), not the browser's IP.2. The Ngrok CORS Error (and WebSocket Error)```textAccess to fetch at 'https://turbine-jitters-chill.ngrok-free.dev/api/models' ... has been blocked by CORS policy...WebSocket connection to 'wss://turbine-jitters-chill.ngrok-free.dev/api/ws' failed```This is a classic ngrok issue! Because you are using a free ngrok account, ngrok intercepts the very first request with an HTML "Warning Page" (saying “You are about to visit an ngrok site…”).Because your React app is expecting JSON data (or a WebSocket handshake) but ngrok returns an HTML warning page instead, it triggers a CORS failure.The Fix is extremely simple:Open a new tab in your browser.Go directly to your ngrok URL: `https://turbine-jitters-chill.ngrok-free.dev`You will see the ngrok warning page. Click the blue "Visit Site" button.Ngrok will now set a cookie in your browser saying you accepted the warning.Go back to your AI-BS Dashboard and refresh. The CORS error and the WebSocket disconnects will instantly vanish!👤 USER REQUEST #24```text<USER_REQUEST>is ngrok needed fore anything im doing at all or is ther a better method if this is needed for anythying?</USER_REQUEST><ADDITIONAL_METADATA>The current local time is: 2026-07-22T13:25:36-04:00.The user's current state is as follows:Active Document: c:\AI-BS\frontend\components\BrettDataTab.jsx (LANGUAGE_JAVASCRIPT)Cursor is on line: 30Other open documents:- c:\AI-BS\frontend\components\BrettDataTab.jsx (LANGUAGE_JAVASCRIPT)- c:\AI-BS\frontend\App.jsx (LANGUAGE_JAVASCRIPT)</ADDITIONAL_METADATA>```---🧠 AGENT REASONING & RESPONSE (Step 267)Ngrok is currently acting as a "bridge" to solve a very specific problem with your architecture, but no, you don't have to use it if you change how you deploy or access your app.Here is exactly why ngrok is currently being used, and the better alternatives available to you:Why is Ngrok there right now?You deployed your frontend dashboard to the public internet using Firebase (`https://ai-bs-dashboard.web.app`). However, your backend server (the Python/Node code that actually fetches data and runs AI models) is still running locally on your physical Windows computer.If you open your live Firebase dashboard on your phone, your phone cannot connect to `localhost` (your computer). Ngrok gives your local computer a temporary public address (`https://turbine-jitters-chill.ngrok-free.dev`) so your public dashboard can talk to your local backend.Better Alternatives (How to get rid of Ngrok)Option 1: The "Local Only" Method (Easiest)If you are the only person using this dashboard and you only use it while sitting at your computer, you don't need Firebase Hosting or Ngrok at all!You can simply run `npm run dev` in your frontend folder and use `http://localhost:5173`.Your local frontend can talk directly to your local backend (`localhost:3001` or `8000`). No ngrok required, and no CORS issues.Option 2: The "Professional Cloud" Method (Best Long-Term)If you want to access your dashboard from your phone, a laptop at a coffee shop, or eventually share it with other users, relying on your home computer to be powered on and running ngrok is a bad idea.The Solution: We would package up your backend (`server.js` and your Python scripts) and deploy them permanently to the cloud using a service like Google Cloud Run, Render, or Heroku.Once deployed, your backend gets a permanent, secure URL (e.g., `https://api.brettstehouwer.com`).We update your frontend to point to that new URL, and you can uninstall ngrok forever!Summary: Ngrok is a temporary development hack. If you want this to be a true, 24/7 universal API tool, our next major project should be migrating your local backend into a real cloud environment! Let me know if you'd like to explore moving the backend to the cloud.👤 USER REQUEST #25```text<USER_REQUEST>Option 2</USER_REQUEST><ADDITIONAL_METADATA>The current local time is: Since you have a Cloudflare account, we can create a Permanent Named Tunnel.This is much better than a "Quick Tunnel" because you can attach it to one of your real domain names (like `api.yourdomain.com`), and the URL will never change. You'll only have to update your frontend code once!Because authenticating with Cloudflare requires opening a browser window that you have to physically click on, you need to run these commands yourself in a PowerShell window.Step 1: Authenticate CloudflaredOpen a new PowerShell terminal, navigate to your backend folder, and run the login command:```powershellcd c:\AI-BS\backend.\cloudflared.exe tunnel login```This will pop up a browser window. Select your Cloudflare account and authorize the tunnel.Step 2: Create the Permanent TunnelOnce authorized, run this to create a tunnel named `ai-bs`:```powershell.\cloudflared.exe tunnel create ai-bs```Take note of the Tunnel UUID it prints out (it will look like `1234-5678-abcd...`), though we usually don't need it right away.Step 3: Route the Tunnel to a DomainPick a subdomain on one of the domains you manage in Cloudflare (for example, `api.yourdomain.com` or `backend.yourdomain.com`) and run this:```powershell.\cloudflared.exe tunnel route dns ai-bs api.yourdomain.com```Step 4: Run the Tunnel!Finally, tell the tunnel to listen to your Python backend (running on port 8000):```powershell.\cloudflared.exe tunnel run --url http://localhost:8000 ai-bs```Let me know when you've got it running (or if you hit any snags)! Once it's running, I'll update your frontend code to use your permanent, lightning-fast, warning-free Cloudflare URL.👤 USER REQUEST #28```text<USER_REQUEST>i need to set up a new site option https://ai-bs-dashboard.web.app/ on cloudflare</USER_REQUEST><ADDITIONAL_METADATA>The current local time is: 2026-07-22T13:32:08-04:00.The user's current state is as follows:Active Document: c:\AI-BS\frontend\components\BrettDataTab.jsx (LANGUAGE_JAVASCRIPT)Cursor is on line: 30Other open documents:- c:\AI-BS\frontend\components\BrettDataTab.jsx (LANGUAGE_JAVASCRIPT)- c:\AI-BS\frontend\App.jsx (LANGUAGE_JAVASCRIPT)</ADDITIONAL_METADATA>```---🧠 AGENT REASONING & RESPONSE (Step 313)Ah, I see! There is a small catch with how Cloudflare works.You cannot add `ai-bs-dashboard.web.app` to your Cloudflare account. The `.web.app` domain is owned by Google/Firebase, so they control the DNS. Cloudflare requires you to own the actual top-level domain (like `brettstehouwer.com` or `ai-bs.com`) to route a permanent tunnel.We Have Two Options:Option A: Do you already own a custom domain?If you own a domain name (like `stehouwer.com`), you can add that domain to Cloudflare, and we can make the tunnel URL something like `api.stehouwer.com`.Option B: Use a Cloudflare Quick Tunnel (Easiest & Free)If you don't have a custom domain, we can skip the Cloudflare account setup entirely and just use a "Quick Tunnel".It works exactly like Ngrok, but without the annoying warning screen (fixing your CORS/WebSocket errors instantly).It gives you a random URL (like `https://rapid-fox-jumped.trycloudflare.com`).The only downside is the URL changes if your PC restarts, meaning you'd have to update your frontend URL.If you just want to get it working right now without buying a domain name, Option B is the way to go.To use Option B, just open PowerShell in `c:\AI-BS\backend` and run this single command:```powershell.\cloudflared.exe tunnel --url http://localhost:8000```It will print out a URL ending in `.trycloudflare.com`. Paste that URL here, and I'll update your frontend to use it!👤 USER REQUEST #29```text<USER_REQUEST>what if i use the existing brettstehouwer.live?</USER_REQUEST><ADDITIONAL_METADATA>The current local time is: 2026-07-22T13:33:11-04:00.The user's current state is as follows:Active Document: c:\AI-BS\frontend\components\BrettDataTab.jsx (LANGUAGE_JAVASCRIPT)Cursor is on line: 30Other open documents:- c:\AI-BS\frontend\components\BrettDataTab.jsx (LANGUAGE_JAVASCRIPT)- c:\AI-BS\frontend\App.jsx (LANGUAGE_JAVASCRIPT)</ADDITIONAL_METADATA>```---🧠 AGENT REASONING & RESPONSE (Step 316)Yes! `brettstehouwer.live` is absolutely perfect. Select your Cloudflare account and authorize it).Step 2: Create the TunnelOnce authorized, create a tunnel named `ai-bs`:```powershell.\cloudflared.exe tunnel create ai-bs```Step 3: Route the DomainTell Cloudflare to route the subdomain `api.brettstehouwer.live` to this new tunnel:```powershell.\cloudflared.exe tunnel route dns ai-bs api.brettstehouwer.live```Step 4: Run It!Finally, start the tunnel and tell it to listen to your Python backend (running on port 8000):```powershell.\cloudflared.exe tunnel run --url http://localhost:8000 ai-bs```Once that is running, let me know! When a local LLM operates in an IDE (like VS Code using extensions such as Continue or Ollama), its "thinking process" is expanded through Agentic Workflows and Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG).How a Local LLM "Thinks" in an IDEWhen you type a prompt or ask for code generation, the model executes a strict, multi-step pipeline:[User Prompt] ➔ [Context Assembly (RAG)] ➔ [Token Prediction Loop] ➔ [Tool Execution]Context Assembly (RAG): The IDE searches your open files, git history, and workspace folders to inject relevant code snippets directly into the hidden system prompt.Token Prediction: The model reads this combined context and calculates probabilities to generate code character by character.Tool Call Parsing: If the model needs external data, it outputs a specific text format (like JSON) that the IDE intercepts to trigger local system tools.The 4 Core Tools of an IDE LLMIn a standard local setup, a single LLM typically relies on 3 to 5 core tools to simulate reasoning and execute tasks.Tool NameWhat it DoesExample ActionWorkspace SearchScans local codebase indexingLocating a function definition in utils.pyFile System APIReads, writes, or modifies filesCreating a new unit test file automaticallyTerminal/Shell ExecutionRuns commands locallyExecuting npm test to verify if code compilesWeb Search / Docs FetcherPulls local API documentationReading a local markdown file or cache for syntaxReal-World Example: Fixing a BugWhen you ask a local LLM to "Fix the broken login function," it orchestrates these tools sequentially:Step 1 (Search): The LLM calls the Workspace Search tool to find login mentions across your codebase.Step 2 (Read): It uses the File System API to read the contents of auth.js.Step 3 (Reason & Write): The model analyzes the syntax errors, predicts the correction, and uses the File System API again to rewrite the file.Step 4 (Verify): It calls the Terminal Tool to run your local test suite and ensure the fix works.Would you like to explore local model setups or see a tool-calling prompt example?12 sitesReasoning about human & LLM reasoning - by Dave FriedmanJul 10, 2023 — We can introspect, analyze our thoughts, and make decisions based on our conscious understanding. In contrast, LLMs don't have con...SubstackHow I Teach LLMs To Think. Most people confuse logical thinking… | by Dr. Derek Austin 🥳 | According to ContextFeb 25, 2026 — Most people confuse logical thinking with “consciousness,” but teaching an LLM to think is just transformer math. Here are the lat...MediumLarge Language Models (LLMs) – FAQ & Guide 20264. Learning Through Probability LLMs don't “think” like humans. Instead, they calculate probabilities. Given a prompt like: “The f...www.credosystemz.comShow allso what other tools does a llm use besides just thinking?To interact with the physical and digital world beyond its internal training data, an LLM uses Function Calling (also known as Tool Use). The model does not execute these tools itself; instead, it outputs structured data (like JSON) specifying a tool name and arguments, which an external application then executes.The 4 Main Tool CategoriesCalculators & Code Interpreters: The model passes mathematical or logical problems to a Python environment to guarantee precise computation.Search Engines & APIs: The LLM queries live databases, web search engines, or weather/stock endpoints to fetch real-time information [finance].Application Connectors: The model triggers actions in external software, such as creating a calendar event, sending an email, or updating a database.Multimodal Perceivers: The LLM utilizes computer vision or text-to-speech tools to process images, record audio, or generate voice responses.Would you like to see a tool-calling JSON code example or learn about specific frameworks like LangChain that enable this?24 sitesLLM Reasoning: Techniques & Examples for Enterprise AIDec 9, 2025 — Tool Use and Function Calling Tool use is an emerging frontier in enhancing LLM reasoning by allowing models to call external func...Heliosz.AIModels - Docs by LangChainJul 20, 2026 — Tool calling Models can request to call tools that perform tasks such as fetching data from a database, searching the web, or runn...Docs by LangChainBuilding AI Agents with Ease: Function Calling in VS Code AI ToolkitAug 15, 2025 — Function calling is a powerful technique that allows Large Language Models (LLMs) to go beyond their static training data and inte...Microsoft Community HubShow allshow me a list of all types of tool categories all oif them that are known to be usedBeyond basic text generation, LLMs use an extensive ecosystem of external tools to interact with data, software, and environments.1. Data Retrieval & Knowledge ToolsWeb Search Engines: Fetches live internet data, news, and current events.Vector Databases (RAG): Searches semantic embeddings of internal company documents or private codebases.Knowledge Graphs: Queries structured, interconnected relational facts (e.g., Wikidata).API Documentation Fetchers: Pulls specialized technical schemas and endpoint definitions on demand.2. Computation & Logic ToolsCode Interpreters: Executes sandboxed code (Python, JavaScript) to solve data science or math problems.Symbolic Calculators: Solves complex algebraic equations precisely (e.g., WolframAlpha) [stem-calculative-problem-solving].SQL/Database Querying: Writes and runs queries directly against relational databases to aggregate metrics.3. File & System OperationsFile System APIs: Reads, creates, appends, and deletes local or cloud-based files.Shell/Terminal Execution: Runs operating system commands, installs dependencies, and manages builds.Web Scrapers / Parsers: Extracts raw text, links, and structured markdown from specific URLs.4. Enterprise & Productivity ConnectorsCommunication APIs: Sends messages, creates channels, and drafts emails (e.g., Slack, Gmail).Calendar & Scheduling: Checks availability, books meetings, and sets reminders.CRM / Project Management: Updates task boards, sales pipelines, and tickets (e.g., Jira, Salesforce).5. Multimodal Generation & ProcessingImage / Video Generators: Creates visual assets based on textual prompt descriptions.Computer Vision Tools: Extracts text (OCR), detects objects, or describes contents of uploaded images.Audio & Speech Tools: Converts speech-to-text (transcription) and text-to-speech (voice synthesis).6. Meta-Cognitive & Agentic ToolsWeb Browsing Agents: Controls a virtual browser to click, scroll, and log into websites autonomously.Memory Modules: Stores long-term user preferences and conversational state across different sessions.Self-Correction Checkers: Routes output through validator models to check for formatting, syntax, or safety errors.Would you like to see a tool-calling JSON schema or examine an agent framework like LangChain?2 sitesExamine Crawler-Based Search Engine Legal Software GuidelinesOct 23, 2025 — Parsing may imply simple hyperlink/URL extraction or it may involve the more complex process of tidying up the HTML content in ord...The Lawyers & Jurists15 most popular generative AI tools shaping 2024 - Businessday NGOct 12, 2024 — While new tools have emerged, some have established themselves as the most popular, serving distinct functions in text, image, and...Business News Nigeriayes\The JSON Tool-Calling SchemaWhen an LLM decides to use a tool, it halts text generation and outputs a structured payload. The application hosting the LLM reads this JSON, runs the code, and passes the result back to the model.Here is the exact schema framework used by developers to register a tool, alongside the JSON output the LLM generates when it wants to execute that tool.1. Tool Declaration (What the LLM reads to know the tool exists)json{  "type": "function",  "function": {    "name": "execute_python_code",    "description": "Runs Python code in a sandboxed environment to solve math or analyze data.",    "parameters": {      "type": "object",      "properties": {        "code": {          "type": "string",          "description": "The exact Python script to execute."        }      },      "required": ["code"]    }  }}Use code with caution.2. Model Tool Call (What the LLM outputs)json{  "id": "call_98x7y6z",  "type": "function",  "function": {    "name": "execute_python_code",    "arguments": "{\"code\": \"import math\\nprint(math.factorial(15))\"}"  }}Use code with caution.3. Tool Response (What the host application feeds back to the LLM)json{  "tool_call_id": "call_98x7y6z",  "role": "tool",  "name": "execute_python_code",  "content": "1307674368000"}Use code with caution.Implementing with Agent Frameworks (LangChain vs. CrewAI)Instead of writing raw JSON setups manually, developers use specialized open-source frameworks to orchestrate these workflows.FrameworkCore Design FocusBest Used ForLangChainSingle-agent chains and sequential data pipelinesConnecting a model to custom APIs or databasesCrewAIMulti-agent roleplay and collaborative teamsComplex workflows requiring multiple specialized botsWould you like to see a Python example using LangChain or build a multi-agent workflow?yesHere is a complete, runnable Python example using LangChain and LangGraph (the modern standard for LangChain agents). This code defines a local tool, binds it to an OpenAI model, and handles the tool-calling loop automatically.Python Code Implementationpythonfrom langchain_core.tools import toolfrom langchain_openai import ChatOpenAIfrom langgraph.prebuilt import create_react_agent# 1. Define a custom tool with explicit type hints and docstrings@tooldef calculate_factorial(n: int) -> str:    """Calculates the factorial of an integer. Use this for complex math."""    import math    try:        return str(math.factorial(n))    except Exception as e:        return f"Error: {str(e)}"# 2. Initialize the LLMllm = ChatOpenAI(model="gpt-4o-mini", temperature=0)# 3. Package the tool into a listtools = [calculate_factorial]# 4. Create the compiled agent executoragent_executor = create_react_agent(llm, tools)# 5. JSONResponse(content=engine_telemetry)# --- Tool Calling Registry API (Phase 3) ---from tools.tool_registry import ToolRegistryclass ToolExecutionPayload(BaseModel):    tool_name: str    arguments: dict = {}@app.get("/api/tools")def list_available_tools():    """Returns declarations of all tools registered for Stehouwer LLM."""    return {"status": "success", "tools": ToolRegistry.get_tool_declarations()}@app.post("/api/tools/execute")def execute_registered_tool(payload: ToolExecutionPayload):    """Executes a registered tool by name with arguments."""    result = ToolRegistry.execute_tool(payload.tool_name, payload.arguments)    return {"status": "success", "result": result}```🧠 AGENT REASONING & RESPONSE (Step 545)🛠️ COMMANDS & TOOL CALLS Created token_estimator.py module```pythonimport reclass HeuristicTokenEstimator:    @staticmethod    def estimate_tokens(text: str) -> int:        if not text:            return 0        words = set(re.findall(r"\b\w+\b", text))        word_count = len(words)        char_count = len(text)        return max(1, int((word_count * 1.3 + char_count / 4) / 2))```🧠 AGENT REASONING & r"C:\Workspaces\Stehouwer_Server\AI-BS_LLM_Core\stehouwer_vector_memory"class ChromaStorageManager:    def __init__(self):        self.client = None        self.collection = None        try:            import chromadb            self.client = chromadb.PersistentClient(path=HOT_DB_PATH)            self.collection = self.client.get_or_create_collection(name="heuristic_resolutions")        except Exception as e:            print(f"[ChromaStorageManager] Fallback mode: {e}")    def query(self, text: str, n_results: int = 5):        if self.collection:            try:                return self.collection.query(query_texts=[text], n_results=n_results)            except Exception as e:                print(f"[ChromaStorageManager] Query error: {e}")        return {"documents": [], "metadatas": []}chroma_manager = ChromaStorageManager()```🧠 AGENT REASONING & RESPONSE (Step 641)🛠️ COMMANDS & TOOL CALLS EXECUTED:Tool Executed: `run_command`Working Directory: Description: Created enterprise_ledger.py module```pythonimport sqlite3import osDB_PATH = os.path.join(os.path.dirname(__file__), "west_michigan.db")class SQLiteAgent:    def __init__(self, db_path=DB_PATH):        self.db_path = db_path        self._init_db()    def _init_db(self):        try:            conn = sqlite3.connect(self.db_path)            cursor = conn.cursor()            cursor.execute("""                CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS ledger (                    id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY AUTOINCREMENT,                    timestamp DATETIME DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP,                    action TEXT,                    details TEXT                )            """)            conn.commit()            conn.close()        except Exception as Executed: `write_to_file`Target File: `c:\AI-BS\backend\token_estimator.py`  Description: Updated HeuristicTokenEstimator init signature```pythonimport reclass HeuristicTokenEstimator:    def __init__(self, max_context_window: int = 6000):        self.max_context_window = max_context_window    @staticmethod    def estimate_tokens(text: str) -> int:        if not text:            return 0        words = set(re.findall(r"\b\w+\b", text))        word_count = len(words)        char_count = len(text)        return max(1, int((word_count * 1.3 + char_count / 4) / 2))```🧠 Instead, we use 3 specialized techniques:1. Dynamic SQL Tool Calling (Recommended & Instant)We add a `query_knowledge_db(sql_query)` tool to your `ToolRegistry`.When you ask Stehouwer LLM a question about your custom knowledge, Stehouwer LLM formulates a `SELECT` query, executes it against your local SQLite database in milliseconds, reads the matching rows, and answers you.Benefits: Zero RAM consumption, works instantly regardless of how many gigabytes the file is, and allows complex filtering/sorting.2. Vector Indexing into ChromaDB (Semantic Search / RAG)We write an ingestion script that extracts text entries from your SQLite tables, generates vector embeddings, and indexes them into your local ChromaDB (`stehouwer_vector_memory`).Benefits: Allows searching by meaning rather than exact keyword matches (e.g. searching for "automobile" will find rows mentioning "car").3. Automatic Schema Inspection & Vault IntegrationWe inspect the database tables, export key documentation/summaries, and place them directly into `BsAi_Knowledge_Vaults` for core persona awareness.---Recommended Path When You Send the FileHere is the safest, most efficient workflow to follow when you're ready:Provide the Path / Zip Location:Place the `.zip` file anywhere on your drive (e.g., `D:\knowledge_db.zip` or `C:\AI-BS\knowledge.zip`).Share the file path with me in your prompt.Automated Unzipping & Inspection:I will use `run_command` to safely unzip the file to a dedicated folder (e.g. `c:\AI-BS\backend\db\stehouwer_knowledge.db`).I will run a quick schema inspection script to analyze table names, column structures, and total row counts.Tool Registration:I will register `query_stehouwer_knowledge_db` as a master tool in `ToolRegistry` so `stehouwer_llm` can query it natively.Safety Verification:I will run a test query to confirm `stehouwer_llm` can read from your SQLite database without any errors or memory pressure on your RTX 4090 GPU.---Ready When You Are!Simply drop the `.zip` file onto your drive, give me the path, and I'll handle the unzipping, schema analysis, and tool integration automatically!👤 USER REQUEST #42```text<USER_REQUEST>the sqlite file is roughly 120 plus gigs. what the best option to Direct SQL Tool Calling with Read-Only Disk Mapping.Here is why this is the ideal path, how it works, and why your PC will handle it effortlessly:---Why SQLite is Built for 120GB+ FilesZero RAM Pressure: SQLite does NOT load 120GB into your system memory. It stays on your SSD and uses B-tree indexing to jump directly to the exact bytes it needs. A query against a 120GB database takes only 5 to 15 milliseconds!Instant Integration: Unlike vector databases (which would take days to embed 120GB of text), SQLite is ready to query the second it is unzipped.Safety & Speed: We open the database in Read-Only Mode (`mode=ro`), so there is zero risk of corrupting your 120GB file while Stehouwer LLM reads from it.---The Recommended 3-Step StrategyStep 1: Storage & UnzippingMake sure you have at least 150GB to 200GB of free space on the drive where you want to extract it (e.g., your `D:\` drive: `D:\Stehouwer_Server\large_knowledge.db`).Step 2: High-Speed Python SQL Tool (`c:\AI-BS\backend\tools\sqlite_tool.py`)We add a specialized tool to `ToolRegistry` called `query_120gb_knowledge_db`.It executes `SELECT` statements with strict `LIMIT` clauses (e.g., `LIMIT 10`) to return precise results back to Stehouwer LLM without blowing up context length.Step 3: Optional Full-Text Search (FTS5) or Index AuditWe run a lightweight 1-minute schema check to verify which columns have indexes (like `CREATE INDEX`). If indexed, searches across 120GB will be virtually instantaneous.---Summary Checklist for YouCheck that your target drive (e.g., `D:\`) has enough free space for the unzipped file.Provide the path to the `.zip` file when you're ready.I will extract it, audit the schema, and connect `stehouwer_llm` to query it natively!👤 USER REQUEST #43```text<USER_REQUEST>provide the best options handling```pythonimport osimport globimport sqlite3import jsonDATABASE_DIR = r"C:\AI-BS\database"def discover_databases(base_dir=DATABASE_DIR):    """Finds all .db, .sqlite, .sqlite3 files inside the target folder recursively."""    extensions = ["*.db", "*.sqlite", "*.sqlite3"]    found_files = []    for ext in extensions:        found_files.extend(glob.glob(os.path.join(base_dir, "**", ext), recursive=True))    return found_filesdef inspect_database_schema(db_path: str):    """Inspects tables, columns, and row counts of a target SQLite database in read-only mode."""    if not os.path.exists(db_path):        return {"status": "error", "message": f"Database file not found at: {db_path}"}    try:        # Open in read-only mode for safety & speed        uri_path = f"file:{db_path}?mode=ro"        conn = sqlite3.connect(uri_path, uri=True)        cursor = conn.cursor()        # Get list of tables        cursor.execute("SELECT name FROM sqlite_master WHERE type='table';")      ```🧠 AGENT REASONING & RESPONSE (Step 857)🛠️ COMMANDS & TOOL CALLS EXECUTED:Tool Executed: `write_to_file`Target File: `c:\AI-BS\backend\tools\tool_registry.py` Core Principles of Local LLM Tool Calling### How an LLM 'Thinks' vs. How Tools WorkAn LLM (Large Language Model) does not have consciousness; it computes probabilistic predictions for the next most logical token. When operating inside an agentic framework like **AI-BS**, its capabilities expand through **Function Calling (Tool Use)**.```[User Request] ➔ [Context Sweep (Living Learning Memory)] ➔ [Ollama Token Prediction] ➔ [JSON Tool Output] ➔ [Backend Execution] ➔ [Synthesized Result]```### The 6 Master Categories of LLM Tools Built into AI-BS:1. **Data Retrieval & Knowledge**: `query_master_memory`, `discover_and_inspect_databases`2. **Computation & Logic**: `execute_sandbox_script` (Python execution)3. **File & System Operations**: `read_sandbox_file`, `write_sandbox_file`4. **Enterprise Connectors**: Firebase & Cloudflare endpoints5. **Multimodal Generation**: `generate_comfy_image` (ComfyUI port 8188), `analyze_image` (LLaVA vision model)6. **Meta-Cognitive & Agentic**: `ScavengerToolAgent` auto-healing background loops---## Phase 1: Baseline Safety & Checkpoint SystemBefore making architectural modifications, we backed up existing working files to ensure zero risk of corruption.```powershell# Backup CommandNew-Item -ItemType Directory -Force -Path 'c:\\AI-BS\\backend\\backups\\pre_tool_upgrade_20260722';Copy-Item -Path 'c:\\AI-BS\\backend\\AI_BS_Backend.py' -Destination 'c:\\AI-BS\\backend\\backups\\pre_tool_upgrade_20260722\\AI_BS_Backend.py';```---Phase 2: Custom Modelfile Persona & System Directive TuningWe configured `stehouwer_llm` in Ollama with system directives, an 8k context window, and JSON tool calling formatting.File Location: `c:\\AI-BS\\backend\\models\\stehouwer_llm.Modelfile````dockerfileFROM llama3.1:latestPARAMETER num_ctx 8192PARAMETER temperature 0.2PARAMETER top_p 0.9PARAMETER stop \"<|start_header_id|>\"PARAMETER stop \"<|end_header_id|>\"PARAMETER stop \"<|eot_id|>\"SYSTEM \"\"\"You are the Stehouwer LLM, the central cognitive intelligence engine of the AI-BS created for Brett Adam Stehouwer.OPERATIONAL DIRECTIVES:1. STABILITY & PRECISION: Provide accurate, zero-fluff, production-ready responses.2. TOOL CALLING: Output JSON function calls when file, code, or memory execution is needed.3. HEURISTIC ALIGNMENT: Prioritize user-validated historical baselines from Living Learning Memory.\"\"\"```Build Command:```bashollama create stehouwer_llm -f c:\\AI-BS\\backend\\models\\stehouwer_llm.Modelfile```---Phase 3: Python Tool Registry ArchitectureFile Location: `c:\\AI-BS\\backend\\tools\\tool_registry.py`The `ToolRegistry` acts as a centralized dispatcher that decodes JSON tool requests emitted by `stehouwer_llm` and executes them inside isolated Python handlers.Exposed Endpoints in `AI_BS_Backend.py`:`GET /api/tools`: Returns declarations for all registered tools.`POST /api/tools/execute`: Executes tools dynamically with parameter validation.---Phase 4: Polyglot Scavenger Engine & Self-Healing LoopFile Location: `c:\\AI-BS\\backend\\tools\\scavenger_tool_agent.py`The `ScavengerToolAgent` runs in the background to automatically detect broken scripts (`*.error`) in the sandbox directory, inspect `master_memory_dump.json` for verified fixes, apply corrected code, and verify that `returncode == 0` before removing the error log.---Phase 5: Multimodal & ComfyUI Tool BindingWe bound local visual generation and vision model capabilities:`generate_comfy_image`: Dispatches prompt payloads to local ComfyUI instance on port 8188.`analyze_image`: Converts uploaded images to Base64 and queries local LLaVA vision model via Ollama.---Phase 6: Dedicated UI Tabs & Firebase Production DeploymentAdded dedicated AI-BS Assistant tab (`ChatTab`) under AI & Data in the Sidebar navigation (`c:\\AI-BS\\frontend\\components\\Sidebar.jsx` & `App.jsx`).Maintained header model selection dropdown across all other tabs.Built production bundle and deployed to Firebase Hosting:```powershellpowershell -ExecutionPolicy Bypass -Command \"npm run build; firebase deploy --only hosting --non-interactive\"```Live URL: https://ai-bs-dashboard.web.app---Phase 7: Handling 200GB+ SQLite Blockchain DatasetsWhy SQLite Scales to 200GB+ Without Crashing RAM:SQLite uses disk-based B-tree indexes and OS memory-mapped I/O (`mmap`).It does NOT load 200GB into RAM. Queries read only the target pages from disk.Read-Only mode (`file:path?mode=ro`) ensures zero corruption risk.Extraction & Inspection Results:Archive Path: `D:\\qbittzdlds\\mainnet.2026-07-01.tar.gz`Extracted Location: `C:\\AI-BS\\database\\blockchain_v2_mainnet.sqlite`Uncompressed Size: 199.45 GBTotal Rows Analyzed:`coin_record`: 413,961,900 rows (413 Million)`full_blocks`: 8,936,286 rows (8.9 Million)`hints`: 46,282,870 rows (46.2 Million)Performance Test Result:Executing a `SELECT` query across 8.9 million block headers on a 200 GB database completed in 2.00 milliseconds!---Key PowerShell & Python Commands Reference1. Live Terminal File Growth Watcher (PowerShell)```powershellwhile ($true) { Clear-Host; $file = Get-Item 'C:\\AI-BS\\database\\blockchain_v2_mainnet.sqlite' -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue; if ($file) { Write-Host (\"🔥 Live Extracted Database Size: {0:N2} GB\" -f ($file.Length / 1GB)) -ForegroundColor Green } else { Write-Host \"Waiting for extraction...\" }; Start-Sleep -Seconds 2 }```2. Python Read-Only SQLite Query Test```pythonimport sqlite3db_path = r'C:\\AI-BS\\database\\blockchain_v2_mainnet.sqlite'conn = sqlite3.connect(f'file:{db_path}?mode=ro', uri=True)cursor = conn.cursor()cursor.execute('SELECT height, in_main_chain FROM full_blocks ORDER BY height DESC LIMIT 5')print(cursor.fetchall())conn.close()```'''with open(output_path, 'w', encoding='utf-8') as f:f.write(markdown_content)print('Saved study guide to:', output_path)"```#### 💻 TERMINAL COMMAND OUTPUT (Step 919)```textCreated At: 2026-07-22T19:19:42ZCompleted At: 2026-07-22T19:19:42Z				The command failed with exit code: 1				Output:				At line:69 char:19+ PARAMETER stop \"<|start_header_id|>\"+                   ~Missing file specification after redirection operator.At line:69 char:18+ PARAMETER stop \"<|start_header_id|>\"+                  ~The '<' operator is reserved for future use.At line:76 char:4+ 1.
